# Demographics of the United States

::: {#o32 .subject cue=lead name="Demographics of the United States"}

With about 4% of the world's population, the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States) is the [third most populous country](wikipedia://en/69058) (after [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India) and [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China)), and the [most populous in the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_in_the_Americas_by_population) and the [Western Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Hemisphere). Its estimated population was 341,784,857 on July 1, 2025, according to the [U.S. Census Bureau](wikipedia://en/57070).[^o2] The country's population grew by 1.8 million, or 0.5%, between 2024 and 2025, due to a decline in net international migration.[^o33] The previous year, the Census Bureau had reported a population increase of 0.98% between 2023 and 2024,[^o34] near the global population growth rate of 1.03%.[^o35] These figures include the [50 states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._state) and the federal capital, [Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington,_D.C.), but exclude the 3.6 million residents of five unincorporated U.S. territories ([Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rico), [Guam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guam), the [U.S. Virgin Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Virgin_Islands), [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Samoa), and the [Northern Mariana Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Mariana_Islands)) as well as several [minor uninhabited island possessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Minor_Outlying_Islands). By several metrics, including racial and ethnic background, religious affiliation, and percentage of rural and urban divide, the state of [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois) is the most representative of the larger demography of the United States.[^o36]

:::

The [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States) population almost quadrupled during the 20th century, with an average growth rate of about 1.3% a year, from about 76 million in 1900 to 281 million in 2000.[^o37] It is estimated to have reached the 200 million mark in 1967, and the 300 million mark on October 17, 2006.[^o37][^o38] Foreign-born immigration caused the U.S. population to continue its rapid increase, with this population doubling from almost 20 million in 1990 to over 45 million in 2015,[^o39] representing one-third of the population increase.[^o40] The U.S. Census Bureau reported in late 2024 that recent immigration to the United States had more than offset the country's lower birth rates: "Net international migration's influence on population trends has increased over the last few years. Since 2021, it accounted for the majority of the nation's growth—a departure from the last two decades, when natural increase was the main factor." This in turn led to an increase in the U.S. population in each of the years 2022, 2023, and 2024 (+0.58%, +0.83%, and +0.98% respectively). Net international migration slowed to a record low in 2025, however, and so did U.S. population growth during that year (+0.5%).[^o41][^o42]

Population growth is fastest among minorities as a whole, and according to a [2020 U.S. Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census) Bureau analysis, 50% of U.S. children under the age of 18 are members of [ethnic minority groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minorities_in_the_United_States).[^o43] As of 2020, [white Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans) numbered 235,411,507 or 71% of the population, including people who identified as white in combination with another race. People who identified as white alone (including [Hispanic whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans)) numbered 204,277,273 or 61.6% of the population, while [non-Latino whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_or_Latino_whites) made up 57.8% of the country's population.[^o44]

[Latino Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latino_Americans) accounted for 51.1% of the country's total [population growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_growth) between 2010 and 2020.[^o45] The Hispanic or Latino population increased from 50.5 million in 2010 to 62.1 million in 2020, a 23% increase and a numerical increase of more than 11.6 million.[^o45] Immigrants and their U.S.-born descendants are expected to provide most of the U.S. population gains in the decades ahead.[^o46]

[Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans) are the fastest-growing racial group in the United States, with a growth rate of 35%. However, multiracial Asian Americans make up the fastest-growing subgroup, with a growth rate of 55%, reflecting the increase of mixed-race marriages in the United States.[^o482][^o481]

According to provisional data, births to [White American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_American) mothers in 2026 remained around 50% of the U.S. total. Since 2020, fertility rates among [Black American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Americans) and [Asian American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans) mothers have continued to decline, and the rates for both have dropped well below the national average. This has led to a slow decline in the total U.S. fertility rate. Nonetheless, the United States continues to report one of the highest fertility rates among high-income countries.[^o47]

*Image: US demographic composition population pyramid.gif.* Population pyramid by race and ethnicity of the United States over time from 1900 to 2020

## Population {#Population}

In 1900, when the U.S. population was 76 million, there were 66.8 million white Americans in the United States, representing 88% of the total population,[^o49] 8.8 million Black Americans, with about 90% of them still living in Southern states,[^o50] and slightly more than 500,000 Hispanics.[^o51]

Under federal law, the [Immigration and Nationality Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hart-Celler_Act),[^o52] the number of first-generation immigrants living in the United States has increased,[^o53] from 9.6 million in 1970 to about 38 million in 2007.[^o54] Around a million people legally immigrated to the United States per year in the 1990s, up from 250,000 per year in the 1950s.[^o55]

In 1900, non-Hispanic whites comprised almost 97% of the population of the 10 largest U.S. [cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Largest_cities_in_the_United_States_by_population_by_decade).[^o56] The Census Bureau reported that minorities (including Hispanic whites) made up 50.4% of the children born in the U.S. between July 2010 and July 2011,[^o57] compared to 37% in 1990.[^o58]

In 2014, the state with the lowest fertility rate was Rhode Island, with a rate of 1.56, while Utah had the greatest rate with a rate of 2.33.[^o200] This correlates with the ages of the states' populations: Rhode Island has the ninth-oldest median age in the US – 39.2 – while Utah has the youngest – 29.0.[^o59]

In 2017, the U.S. birth rate remains well below the replacement level needed – at least 2.1 children per woman so as not to experience population decreases – as white American births fell in all 50 states and the District of Columbia. Among non-Hispanic white women, no states had a fertility rate above the replacement level. Among non-Hispanic Black women, 12 states reached above the replacement level needed. Among Hispanic women, 29 states did.[^o60] For non-Hispanic white women, the highest total fertility rate was in Utah, at 2.099, and the lowest in the District of Columbia, at 1.012. Among non-Hispanic Black women, the highest total fertility rate was in Maine, at 4.003, and the lowest in Wyoming, at 1.146. For Hispanic women, the highest total fertility rate was in Alabama, at 3.085, and the lowest in Vermont, at 1.200, and Maine, at 1.281.[^o60][^o61] As of 2016, due to aging, low birth rates and rising mortality [driven partly by drug overdoses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_epidemic_in_the_United_States), deaths outnumber births among [non-Hispanic whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_whites) in more than half the states in the country.[^o62]

#### Sex distribution {#Sex_distribution}

The 2020 U.S. census reported there were more females than males with females making up 50.9% (or 168,763,470 people) of the population and males making up 49.1% (or 162,685,811 people). The previous census in 2010 also reported that there were more females than males; but females made up slightly less of the population at 50.8% and males made slightly more at 49.2%.[^o67]

The first U.S. census to report more females than males was the 1950 census.[^o68]

### Dependency ratio {#Dependency_ratio}

*Image: Comparison of ages in the Contiguous United States by county, 2020.jpg.* Bi-variate choropleth map comparing the estimated percent of the population 65 and older and 17 and younger in the Contiguous United States by county, 2020

The [dependency ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dependency_ratio) is the age-population ratio of people who are normally not in the labor force (the dependent population, which includes those aged 0 to 14 and 65 and older) to those who are (the productive part, ages 15 to 64). It is used to gauge the strain on the populace that is productive. The support ratio is the ratio of the working-age population to the elderly population, that is, the reciprocal of the aged dependency ratio.

| Potential support ratio | 160th | [^o76] |

:::

### Density {#Density}

The most densely populated state is [New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey) (1,263/mi<sup>2</sup> or 488/km<sup>2</sup>).

The population is highly [urbanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization_in_the_United_States), with 83.3% of the population residing in cities and suburbs.[^o7] Large urban clusters are spread throughout the eastern half of the United States (particularly the Great Lakes area, northeast, east, and southeast) and the western tier states; mountainous areas, principally the Rocky Mountains and Appalachian chain, deserts in the southwest, the dense boreal forests in the extreme north, and the central prairie states are less densely populated; Alaska's population is concentrated along its southern coast – with particular emphasis on the city of [Anchorage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchorage) – and Hawaii's is centered on the island of Oahu.[^o7] [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California) and [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas) are the most populous states, as the [mean center of U.S. population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mean_center_of_U.S._population) has consistently shifted westward and southward.[^o80][^o81] [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City) is the [most populous city in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population)[^o82] and has been since [at least 1790](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most_populous_cities_in_the_United_States_by_decade#1790).

In the [U.S. territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territories_of_the_United_States), population centers include the [San Juan metro area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan-Caguas-Guaynabo_metropolitan_area) in [Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rico),[^o83] [Saipan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saipan) in the [Northern Mariana Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Mariana_Islands),[^o84] and the island of [Tutuila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tutuila) in [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Samoa).[^o85]

::: {#o87 .pointer-item cue=pointer name="United states median age"}

### Median age of the population {#Median_age_of_the_population}

*Image: Median Age by County 2022.webp.* **Median Age by County 2022** \#007A67 \#009787 \#00B2A4 \#80D2C9 \#E6F4F7

The [median age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_age) of the total population as of 2021 is 38.8 years; the male median age is 37.7 years; the female median age is 39.8 years.[^o76]

### Population centers {#Population_centers}

The United States has dozens of major cities, including 31 ["global cities"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_city)[^o93] of all types, with 10 in the "alpha" group of global cities: [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City), [Los Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles), [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago), [Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington,_D.C.), [Boston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston), [San Francisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco), [Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami), [Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia), [Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas), and [Atlanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta).[^o94] , the United States had 56 metropolitan areas with 1 million or more inhabitants. (The U.S. Census Bureau ranked Urban Honolulu as the 56th most populous area, with just over 1 million residents.)

, about 250 million Americans live in or around urban areas. That means more than three-quarters of the U.S. population shares just about three percent of the U.S. land area.[^o95]

:::

### Population by year (includes estimates) {#Population_by_year_(includes_estimates)}

This table includes the official United States population by year from the [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Census_Bureau), and includes the Bureau's intercensal estimates. Such estimates are taken on July 1 of each year but are not included for the years of the decennial census (2000, 2010, 2020, etc.). Whether the figure is a decennial census or an intercensal estimate is noted.

## Vital statistics {#Vital_statistics}

### U.S. demographic table {#U.S._demographic_table}

Sources: population,[^o99][^o100][^o101][^o102] births and deaths,[^o103][^o104][^o105][^o106] TFR,[^o107][^o108][^o109] IMR,[^o110][^o111][^o112][^o113] life expectancy[^o114]

Notable events in American demographics:

- 1846–1851: [Irish Immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Americans) during the [Great Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Famine_%28Ireland%29)
- 1861–1865: [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War)
- 1874–1879: [Long Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Depression)
- 1917–1918: [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I) and [Spanish flu pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_flu)
- 1929–1939: [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression)
- 1941–1945: [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II)
- 1946–1964: [mid-20th-century baby boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-20th-century_baby_boom)
- 1957–1958: [Asian flu pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957–1958_influenza_pandemic)
- 2008: [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) and [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession)
- 2020–2023: [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_States)
- 2022–2024: [Mexico–United States border crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico–United_States_border_crisis#Biden_administration_%282021–2025%29)

*Image: Families US.png.* Marriages, Families and Intimate Relationships in the U.S., 1970–2000

*Image: Median age at first marriage in the US.svg.* Median age at first marriage in the U.S.

According to the [U.S. Census Bureau](wikipedia://en/57070), in 2021, the population of the United States grew at a slower rate than in any other year since the country's founding.[^o120] The U.S. population grew only 0.1% from the previous year before.[^o120] The U.S. population has grown by less than one million people for the first time since 1937, with the lowest numeric growth since at least 1900, when the Census Bureau began yearly population estimates.[^o120] Apart from the previous few years, when population growth plummeted to historically low levels, the slowest pace of increase in the twentieth century occurred between 1918 and 1919, when the [influenza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Influenza) epidemic and [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I) were both in full swing.[^o120] Slower population growth has been the norm in the United States for some years, owing to lower fertility and net [international migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_migration), as well as rising mortality from an aging population.[^o120]

To put it another way, since the mid-2010s, births and net international migration have been dropping while deaths have risen. These trends have a cumulative effect of reduced population increase.[^o120]

The [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic) has accelerated this trend, resulting in a historically slow population increase in 2021.

The growth rate is 0.1% as estimated for 2021.[^o120]

The birth rate is 11.0 births/1,000 population, as of 2020.[^o121] This was the lowest birth rate since records began. There were 3,613,647 births in 2020, this was the lowest number of births since 1980.[^o121]

- \11.0 births/1,000 population per year (final data for 2020).
- \11.4 births/1,000 population per year (final data for 2019).[^o121]

In 2020, the [CDC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centers_for_Disease_Control_and_Prevention) reported that there were 1,676,911 [marriages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_in_the_United_States) in 2020, compared to 2019, there were 2,015,603 marriages.[^o122] Marriage rates varied significantly by state, ranging from 3.2 marriages/1,000 population in California to 21.0 marriages/1,000 population in Nevada.\*[^o123]

- \5.1 marriages/1,000 population per year (provisional data for 2020).[^o122]
- \6.1 marriages/1,000 population per year (provisional data for 2019).[^o122]

\*Rates are based on provisional counts of marriages by state of occurrence

In 2009, *[Time magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_magazine)* reported that 40% of births in the United States were to unmarried women.[^o124] The following is a breakdown by race for unwed births: 17% Asian, 29% White, 53% Hispanics (of any race), 66% Native Americans, and 72% Black American.[^o125]

According to the CDC, in 2020, there were at least 1,461,121 births to unmarried women, or 40.5% of all births in the United States. The following is a breakdown by race for unwed births: 28.4% Non-Hispanic White, 70.4% Non-Hispanic Black, and 52.8% Hispanic (of any race).[^o126]

The drop in the U.S. birth rate from 2007 to 2009 is believed to be associated with the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession).[^o127]

A study by the [Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agency_for_Healthcare_Research_and_Quality) (AHRQ) found that more than half (51 percent) of live hospital births in 2008 and 2011 were male.[^o128]

Per U.S. federal government data released in March 2011, births fell 4% from 2007 to 2009, the largest drop in the U.S. for any two-year period since the 1970s.[^o129] Births have declined for three consecutive years, and are now 7% below the peak in 2007.[^o130] This drop has continued through 2010, according to data released by the U.S. National Center for Health Statistics in June 2011.[^o130] Numerous experts have suggested that this decline is largely a reflection of unfavorable economic conditions.[^o131] This connection between birth rates and economic downturns partly stems from the fact that American birth rates have now fallen to levels that are comparable to the Great Depression of the 1930s.[^o132] Teen birth rates in the U.S. are at their lowest level in U.S. history.[^o133] In fact, teen birth rates in the U.S. have consistently decreased since 1991 through 2011, except for a brief increase between 2005 and 2007.[^o133] The other aberration from this otherwise steady decline in teen birth rates is the 6% decrease in birth rates for 15- to 19-year-olds between 2008 and 2009.[^o133] Despite these years of decrease, U.S. teen birth rates still remain higher than in other developed nations.[^o133] Racial differences prevail with teen birth and pregnancy rates as well. American Indian/Alaska Native, Hispanic, and non-Hispanic Black teen pregnancy rates are more than double the non-Hispanic white teen birth rate.[^o134]

:::

### Total fertility rate (TFR) {#Total_fertility_rate_(TFR)}

*Image: Fertility rate of the United States from 1820 to 2016.svg.* TFR of the United States overtime from 1820 to 2016

In 1800 the average U.S. woman had 7.04 children;[^o137] by the first decade of the 1900s, this number had already decreased to 3.56.[^o138] Since 1971, the birth rate has generally been [below the replacement rate of 2.1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-replacement_fertility).[^o139][^o140]p. 3 Since the [Great Recession of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession), the rate has consistently been below replacement.[^o139][^o140]p. 3 The drop in the TFR from 2.08 per woman in 2007 to 1.76 in 2017 was mostly due to the declining birth rate of ethnic minorities, teenagers and women in their 30s.[^o141] During that period, the birthrate for women ages 35 to 44 has risen.[^o139][^o141] The 12 month ending general fertility rate increased from 56.6 to 57.0 in 2022 Q1 compared to 2021 Q4.[^o142]

#### Total fertility rates from 1800 to 2020 {#Total_fertility_rates_from_1800_to_2020}

The [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) is the number of children born per woman. Sources: [Ansley J. Coale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansley_J._Coale), Zelnik and [National Center for Health Statistics](wikipedia://en/1042734).[^o144]

::: {#o145 .table cue=table}

| Years | 1800 | 1810 | 1820 | 1830 | 1840 | 1850 | 1860 | 1870 | 1880 | 1890 | 1900[^o144] |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Total Fertility Rate in the United States | \7.0 | \6.9 | \6.7 | \6.6 | \6.1 | \5.4 | \5.2 | \4.6 | \4.2 | \3.9 | \3.6 |

:::

::: {#o146 .table cue=table}

| Years | 1910 | 1920 | 1930 | 1940 | 1950 | 1960 | 1970 | 1980 | 1990 | 2000 | 2010[^o144] | 2020 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Total Fertility Rate in the United States | \3.4 | \3.2 | \2.5 | \2.2 | \3.0 | \3.5 | \2.5 | \1.8 | \2.08 | \2.06 | \1.93 | \1.64 |

:::

The U.S. total [fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertility_rate) as of 2020 is 1.641[^o121]

- \1.55 for non-Hispanic whites[^o121]
- \1.71 for non-Hispanic Blacks[^o121]
- \1.65 for [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) (including Hispanics)
- \1.53 for [Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans) (including Hispanics)

Other:[^o200]

- \1.88 for *[Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans)* (of all racial groups)[^o121]

(Note that ≈95% of Hispanics are included as "white Hispanics" by CDC, which does not recognize the census's "Some other race" category and counts people in that category as white.)

Source: National Vital statistics report based on [2010 US census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_US_census) data[^o483]

#### Total fertility rates by state, federal district or territory {#Total_fertility_rates_by_state,_federal_district_or_territory}

### Births and fertility by race {#Births_and_fertility_by_race}

A total of 3,659,289 babies were born in 2021, a 1% increase from 2020. Additionally, researchers also looked at births by race and found that White and Hispanic women each saw the number of births increase by about 2% from 2020 to 2021. Meanwhile, Black and Asian women saw the number of births decline by 2.4% and 2.5%, respectively, over the same period, while American Indian/Alaskan Native women saw their numbers fall by 3.2%.[^o149] It also marks the first rise in births since 2014. Prior to this report, the total number of births had been decreasing by an average of 2% per year.[^o149] However, the [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) (the number of births that the average women have over their lifetimes) was 1.6635 births per every woman. This is still below the replacement level, the level a population needs to replace itself, which is, at least, 2.1 births per woman.[^o149]

### Life expectancy {#Life_expectancy}

*Image: Life-expectancy, 1880 to 2023, USA.svg.* Life expectancy in the United States since 1880

*Image: Life expectancy by WBG -USA -diff.png.* Life expectancy in the United States since 1960 by gender

According to the [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centers_for_Disease_Control_and_Prevention) (CDC), average American [life expectancy](wikipedia://en/18669) at birth was 79.0 years in 2024, a record high.[^o217] This was a gain of 0.6 years from 78.4 years in 2023.[^o218] Average life expectancy was 76.4 years in 2021.[^o219]p. 1

- Male: 76.5 (2024), 75.8 years (2023), 74.8 years (2022), 73.5 years (2021)[^o220]
- Female: 81.4 years (2024), 81.1 years (2023), 80.2 years (2022), 79.3 years (2021)[^o221]

Starting in 1998, life expectancy in the U.S. fell [behind that of other wealthy industrialized countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy), and Americans' "health disadvantage" gap has been increasing ever since.[^o222] Average U.S. life expectancy in the United States has actually declined in four of the years following 2014 (the year when average U.S. life expectancy reached 78.9 years, its historical peak).[^o223] These declines were mostly reversed in 2022 (+1.1 years), 2023 (+0.9 years), and 2024 (+0.6 years). The CDC attributed the improvement in 2024 to a significant fall in the number of fatal drug overdoses, adding that "heart disease continues to be the leading cause of death in the United States, followed by cancer and unintentional injuries."[^o224][^o225] As of 2024, death rates among the [youngest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_mortality) in the U.S. remain well higher than in peer nations.[^o225] In 2023, there had already been lower death rates in each of the ten U.S. [leading causes of death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_causes_of_death_by_rate), with gains in life expectancy largely driven by "decreases in mortality due to COVID-19, heart disease, unintentional injuries, cancer and diabetes".[^o225][^o226]

From 2019 to 2020, the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic) had contributed to approximately 61% of the decrease in life expectancy in the United States.[^o240] While increases in mortality from [unintentional injuries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unintentional_injuries), [heart disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease), [homicide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homicide), and [diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes) contributed to 11.7%, 5.8%, 2.9%, and 2.8% of the decrease in life expectancy from 2019 to 2020, respectively.[^o240] Life expectancy has also varied by racial and ethnic group, with Non-Hispanic Asians having the highest life expectancy and Non-Hispanic American Indians having the lowest.[^o240] In 2021, life expectancy at birth in the United States fell for the second year in a row, the first two-year drop since 1961–1963.[^o227]

## Future projections {#Future_projections}

In 2023, the U.S. Census Bureau published an updated projection of the U.S. population for 2023–2100, taking into account the results of the [2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census). The future size and composition of the U.S. population were estimated using the cohort-component method and historical trends in fertility, mortality, and international migration.[^o242] The projection included a main variant and three alternative variants reflecting different international migration scenarios ("high immigration", "low immigration", and "zero immigration"), since immigration is the most uncertain component in population forecasting.[^o243]

## Race, ethnicity, & other groups {#Race,_ethnicity,_&_other_groups}

*Image: Complete history of the Racial and ethnic demographics of the United States in percentage of the population.svg.*

The following table shows the race and ethnicity of the United States per the 1930, 1970, 2000, and 2020 censuses. Data only covers states and the federal district, thus only covering the [first 48 states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contiguous_United_States) and [Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington,_D.C.) in 1930 and including [Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska) and [Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii) as well in 1970, 2000, and 2020. The figures thus do not include various other territories that have been under the United States during this time period. Over this time period, the U.S. has changed from being 89% White, 10% Black and 1% Hispanic in 1930 to 58% White, 12% Black, and 19% Hispanic ninety years later, reflecting a significant demographic shift.

### Race {#Race}

*Image: Population pyramid of the United States by race-ethnicity in 2020.svg.* Population pyramid by race/ethnicity in 2020

The [United States Census Bureau](wikipedia://en/57070) collects racial data in accordance with guidelines provided by the U.S. Office of Management and Budget (OMB), and these data are based on self-identification. Many other countries count multiple races based on origin while America compiles multiple dozens of ethnicity groups into skin color grouping them together.[^o275] The racial classifications and definitions used by the U.S. Census Bureau are:[^o276]

- [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans): a person having origins in any of the original peoples of Europe, the Middle East, or North Africa.[^o277] It includes people who indicate their race as "White" or report entries such as English, Azerbaijani, Iranian (Kurd and Lur), Irish, German, Italian, Spanish, Portuguese, Greek, Turkish, Lebanese, Moroccan (Arab and Berber), or Caucasian.
- [Black or African American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans): a person having origins in any of the Black racial groups of Africa.[^o277] It includes people who indicate their race as "Black, African Am." or report entries such as African American, Kenyan, Nigerian, or Haitian.
- [American Indian or Alaska Native](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States): a person having origins in any of the original peoples of North and South America (including Central America) and who maintains tribal affiliation or community attachment.[^o277] This category includes people who indicate their race as "American Indian or Alaska Native" or report entries such as Navajo, Blackfeet, Inupiat, Yup'ik, Central American Indian groups, or South American Indian groups.
- [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans): a person having origins in any of the original peoples of East Asia, Southeast Asia, or South Asia, such as Cambodia, China, India, Japan, Korea, Malaysia, Pakistan, the Philippine Islands, Thailand, and Vietnam.[^o277]
- [Native Hawaiian or Other Pacific Islander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islander_Americans): a person having origins in any of the original peoples of Hawaii, Guam, Samoa, or other Pacific Islands.[^o277]
- Some other race: includes all other responses not included in the "White", "Black or African American", "American Indian or Alaska Native", "Asian", and "Native Hawaiian or other Pacific Islander" racial categories described above includes Asians from West Asia or Russia (non-European Russia) and White Africans.
- [Two or more races](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial_Americans): people may choose to provide two or more races either by checking two or more race response check boxes, providing multiple responses, or some combination of check boxes and other responses.

Data about race and ethnicity are self-reported to the Census Bureau. Since the 2000 census, Congress has authorized people to identify themselves according to more than one racial classification by selecting more than one category. Only one ethnicity may be selected, however, because the Census Bureau recognizes only two ethnicities – "Hispanic or Latino" and "Not Hispanic or Latino" – which are mutually exclusive since you can be one or the other, but not both. The singular term Hispanic has been supplanted as a federally-recognized ethnicity by the combined "Hispanic or Latino," defined by the Census Bureau as a person of Cuban, Mexican, Puerto Rican, Cuban, South or Central American, or other Spanish culture or origin, regardless of race.[^o278]

*Image: Largest\_Race\_and\_Ethnicity\_in\_United\_States\_(2020).jpg.* Map of counties in United States by racial and ethnic plurality, per the 2020 U.S. census

According to the Census Bureau website, the racial composition of the United States in 2021 was:[^o279]

::: {#o280 .table cue=table}

| Race (2021) | Population | % of population |
|---|---|---|
| Total | 323,175,700 | \100.0% |
| (Non-Hispanic) [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans) | 187,925,100 | \58.2% |
| (Non-Hispanic) [Black or African American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans) | 37,520,800 | \11.6% |
| [Hispanic or Latino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) | 61,241,900 | \19.0% |
| (Non-Hispanic) [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans) | 18,558,600 | \5.7% |
| (Non-Hispanic) North American Native | 1,667,100 | \0.5% |
| (Non-Hispanic) Pacific Islander | 541,200 | \0.2% |
| Two or more Races | 15,711,100 | \4.9% |

:::

### Hispanic or Latino origin {#Hispanic_or_Latino_origin}

*Image: CensusViewer US 2010 Census Latino Population as Heatmap by Census Tract.jpg.* CensusViewer US 2010 census Latino Population as a heatmap by census tract

The U.S. Office of Management and Budget (OMB) defines "Hispanic or Latino" as a person of Cuban, Mexican, Puerto Rican, Dominican, South or Central American, or other Spanish culture or origin regardless of race. People who identify with the terms "Hispanic" or "Latino" are those who classify themselves in one of the specific Hispanic or Latino categories listed on the decennial census questionnaire and various Census Bureau survey questionnaires – "Mexican, Mexican Am., Chicano" or "Puerto Rican" or "Cuban" – as well as those who indicate that they are "another Hispanic, Latino, or Spanish origin".[^o426] People who identify their origin as Hispanic or Latino may be of any race.[^o276]

### Indigenous peoples {#Indigenous_peoples}

As of 2020, there are 9,666,058 people identifying as [American Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) and [Alaska Native](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Native) people in the United States, including those identifying with more than one race,[^o431] representing around 3% of the U.S. population. This number includes not only groups indigenous to the United States, but any [Indigenous people of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas),[^o432] including [Mesoamerican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesoamerica) peoples such as the [Maya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_peoples), as well as [Canadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_in_Canada) and [South American natives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_South_America). In 2022, 634,503 Indigenous people in the United States identified with Central American Indigenous groups, 875,183 identified with the [Indigenous people of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_Mexico), and 47,518 identified with Canadian [First Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nations_in_Canada).[^o433] Of the 3.2 million Americans who identified as American Indian or Alaska Native alone in 2022, around 45% were of [Hispanic or Latino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) ethnicity,[^o434][^o435] with this number growing as increasing numbers of Indigenous people from Latin American countries immigrate to the U.S. and more Latinos self-identify with indigenous heritage. Of groups Indigenous to the contiguous United States, the largest self-reported tribes are [Cherokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee) (1,449,888), [Navajo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo) (434,910), Choctaw (295,373), [Blackfeet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackfeet_Nation) (288,255), and [Sioux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sioux) (220,739). Additionally, 205,954 identify with an [Alaska Native](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Natives) tribe. There are 573 [federally recognized tribal governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Native_American_Tribal_Entities)[^o436] in the United States.[^o437]

The U.S. Census Bureau classifies Native Hawaiians separately from American Indians and Alaska Natives, grouping them with [Pacific Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islander_Americans) instead. According to 2022 estimates, 714,847 Americans identified with Native Hawaiian ancestry.[^o438]

### Other groups {#Other_groups}

#### Veterans {#Veterans}

There were 15.8 million [veterans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veteran) in 2023,[^o441] with 6.2% of Americans having served in the [Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces).[^o442] In 2023 the war with the highest number of veterans was the Korean War. Most veterans were male at 14 million and 1.7 million veterans were female.[^o441] Currently the veteran population in the United States is getting smaller but more diverse. The racial makeup of veterans was in 2023 as follows: 74.1% white, 12.6% African-American, 10.3% other/mixed-race, 2.2% Asian/Native Hawaiian/Pacific Islander and 0.8% being Native American.[^o443] Veterans have a lower poverty and unemployment than non-veterans but have higher rates of disability than non-veterans.[^o444]

#### Illegal immigrants {#Illegal_immigrants}

In 2010, *[The Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Post)* estimated that there were 11 million illegal immigrants in the country.[^o446] In 2017, the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) reported an estimated 10.5 million illegal immigrants in the U.S.[^o447] In 2025, Pew announced that "the number of unauthorized immigrants in the United States reached an all-time high of 14 million in 2023 after two consecutive years of growth".[^o448]

#### Prisoners {#Prisoners}

In 2024, an estimated 1,867,404 adults were imprisoned in the United States according to the 2024 American Community Survey.[^o450]

In 2016 it was reported that 92.7% of all prisoners were male, 6.9% female, 0.3% transgender, and 0.1% nonbinary.[^o451] In 2022, there were 180,684 women incarcerated in U.S. prisons and jails. Most women involved in the country's correctional system were actually under probation, with 717,811 being so classified. Female parolees numbered 76,870, while 87,874 women were held in prisons and 92,900 women were held in jails in 2022.[^o452]

Some 37% of all federal prisoners were Hispanic, 32% Black/African American, 21% white, and 2% Native American or Alaska Native. Asians, Native Hawaiians and other Pacific islanders made up 2% of the population, while "nearly 7% of federal prisoners identified as two or more races". In state prisons, 34% identified as Black/African American, 32% white, 21% Hispanic, 1% Native American or Alaskan Native, 1% as being either Asian/Native Hawaiian/Other Pacific Islander, and 11% "identified with two or more races".[^o451]

#### LGBTQ Population {#LGBTQ_Population}

The United States Census Bureau does not measure [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation) but plans are in place to start measuring it starting in 2027 with the [American Community Survey](wikipedia://en/592043).[^o454] In 2024, Gallup reported that 9.3% of adults were [LGBTQ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_people). The percentage of people who have reported being LGBTQ has risen during the 2010s and 2020s. Of LGBTQ adults in the United States, the most common identification was [bisexual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bisexuality_in_the_United_States), with 56.3% of LGBTQ adults identifying as bisexual; bisexual people made up 5.2% of the adult U.S. population. The survey also reported that 13.9% of LGBTQ adults were [transgender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender) and made up 1.3% of the U.S. adult population.[^o455]

In the 2020 United States census, there were more reported [same-sex married couples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage) than unmarried ones as there were 668,497 married same-sex couples and 500,073 unmarried same-sex couples.[^o456]

In June 2023, UCLA's Williams Institute reported that there were approximately 823,000 [same-sex couples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_relationship) in the United States. Also according to the institute, most same-sex couples were female at 53% and males were 47% of same-sex couples.[^o457]

### Projections {#Projections}

*U.S. Census Population projections (2023)[^o460]*

::: {#o459 .table cue=table name="U.S. Census Population projections (2023)"}

|  | 2023 | 2060 |
|---|---|---|
| [White Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_American)<sup>1</sup> | \75.5% | \72.3% |
| \> [Non-Hispanic Whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_Whites) | *\58.9%* | *\44.9%* |
| [Black Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_American)<sup>1</sup> | \13.6% | \14.8% |
| [Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_American)<sup>1</sup> | \6.3% | \9.4% |
| [Multiracial Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial_American)<sup>1</sup> | \3.0% | \6.1% |
| [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States)<sup>1</sup> | \1.3% | \1.4% |
| [Pacific Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islander_Americans)<sup>1</sup> | \0.3% | \0.4% |
| *[Hispanics/Latinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans)* (of any race) | *\19.1%* | *\26.9%* |
| <sup>1</sup> *Including Hispanics* |  |  |

:::

A report by the [U.S. Census Bureau](wikipedia://en/57070) [projects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_population_projections) a decrease in the ratio of non-Hispanic Whites between 2023 and 2060, a decline from 58.9% of the population to 44.9%. [Non-Hispanic Whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_Whites) are projected to no longer make up a majority of the population by 2050, but will remain the largest single ethnic group. Non-Hispanic whites made up 85% of the population in 1960.[^o469]

While non-Hispanic whites are projected to become a minority, the total White population (including Hispanics), will remain a majority from 2023 to 2060, falling from 75.5% to 72.3% of the population who are white alone according to the projections.[^o470] However, these projections are not directly comparable to other Census Bureau data, as they are based on a modified race dataset,[^o471] which does not include the "[some other race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Some_Other_Race)" category used in census surveys.[^o472] Individuals identifying as "some other race" alone or in combination made up 16.2% of the population in 2022,[^o473] and they are reclassified into recognized race categories in the dataset used for the projections.[^o474] As a result, there is a significant discrepancy between the share of the white alone population in 2023 according to the projections (75.5%), and the estimated share of white alone (60.9%), as reported by the American Community Survey in 2022.[^o475]

The report foresees the Hispanic or Latino population rising from 19.1% today to 26.9% by 2060, the Black percentage barely rising from 13.6% to 14.8%, and Asian Americans upping their 6.3% share to 9.4%. The United States had a population of 333 million people in July 2023, and is projected to reach 355 million by 2040 and 364 million in 2060.[^o476][^o497][^o477][^o478][^o479] It is further projected that all of the increase in population from 2023 to 2060 will be due to [immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_the_United_States).

Of the nation's children in 2060, 64% are expected to be of a minority ethnicity, up from 51% today. Approximately 32% are projected to be Hispanic or Latino (up from 26% in 2023), and 36% are projected to be single-race, non-Hispanic Whites (down from 49% in 2023). Racial and ethnic minorities surpassed non-Hispanic whites as the largest group of U.S. children under 5 years old in 2015.[^o480]

The fastest growing racial group in America is [Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans) with a growth rate of 35%, however the multiracial mixed Asian group is growing even faster, with a growth rate of 55%. Multiracial Asians are therefore the fastest growing demographic group in America.[^o481][^o482]

In 2020, it was reported that 51.0% of births were to non-Hispanic white mothers.[^o483] In 2021, the percentage increased to 51.5%.[^o483][^o484] However, by 2022 the rate of births to white mothers had declined by 3%, dropping to 50% of all total births. In the same period, the rate of births to Asian and Hispanic women increased by 2% and 6%, respectively.[^o485] In the same time period, births to Asian American and Hispanic women increased by 2% and 6%, respectively.[^o486]

**Pew Research Center projections**

The [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations) projects a population of just over 400 million in 2060.

*[Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) projections (2008)[^o488]*

::: {#o487 .table cue=table name="Pew Research Center projections (2008)"}

|  | 1960 | 2005 | 2050 |
|---|---|---|---|
| [White Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_American) | 85% | 67% | 47% |
| [Hispanic Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_American) | \3.5% | 14% | 29% |
| [Black Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_American) | 11% | 13% | 14% |
| [Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_American) | \0.6% | 5% | 9% |
| Note: All races modified and not Hispanic; American Indian/Alaska Native not shown. |  |  |  |

:::

The country's racial profile will be vastly different, and although whites will remain the single largest ethnic group in the U.S., they will no longer be a majority excluding [White Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) by 2055 according to [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center). Growth in the Hispanic and Asian populations is predicted to almost triple over the next 40 years. By 2055, the breakdown is estimated to be 48% non-Hispanic white, 24% Hispanic, 16% Black, and 14% Asian.[^o493]

, 14% of the United States' population is foreign born, compared to just 5% in 1965. Nearly 39 million immigrants have come to the U.S. since 1965, with most coming from Asia and Latin America. The 2015 Census Report predicts that the percentage of the U.S. population that is foreign-born will continue to increase, reaching 19% by 2060. This increase in the foreign-born population will account for a large share of the overall population growth.[^o493]

The average person in the U.S. of 2060 is likely to be older than the average person of 2018 today, and it is projected that almost one in four people will be 65 or older.[^o493]

## Religion {#Religion}

### Religious affiliations {#Religious_affiliations}

The table below is based mainly on selected data as reported to the [United States Census Bureau](wikipedia://en/57070). It only includes the voluntary self-reported membership of religious bodies with 750,000 or more. The definition of a member is determined by each religious body.[^o646] , the US census bureau reported that about 13% of the population did not identify themselves as a member of any religion.[^o647]

According to data from Pew Research, Americans are significantly more religious on average than populations in other developed [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_world) nations, with 55% of Americans reporting praying daily, compared to only 25% of Canadians, 18% of Australians, 6% of British people, and 22% of Europeans as a whole.[^o648] The country has a smaller share of unaffiliated or atheist population than most other Western nations. However, this population has been growing significantly in recent decades. Surveys conducted in 2014 and 2019 by Pew indicated that the percentage of Americans unaffiliated with a religion increased from 16% in 2007 to 23% in 2014 and 26% of the population in 2019.[^o649][^o650] A Pew Research Survey performed in 2012 found that the number of Americans without a religion was approaching the number of Evangelical Protestants, and estimated that if the current growth rate in irreligion continued, around 51% of Americans will not have a religion by 2050.[^o651]

According to statistical data made by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) in 2023 about 62% of the US population is Christian, 29% is Unaffiliated, 2% is Jewish, 1% follows Buddhism, 1% follows Hinduism, 1% follows Islam and 3% follow traditional religions and others.[^o652] Currently, the United States has the largest Christian population in the world (approximately 210 million) and the largest [Protestant Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_the_United_States) population (approximately 130 million). The country also has the second largest Jewish community in the world (after Israel) and the largest Buddhist and Hindu communities in the West, as well as the largest number of followers of Islam in North America. The country has about 99 million non-affiliates (only China and Japan have more).

According to [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) study released in 2018, by 2040, Islam will surpass Judaism to become the second largest religion in the US due to higher immigration and birth rates.\<ref name=Islamization1[^o765]

### Religions of U.S. adults {#Religions_of_U.S._adults}

The United States government does not collect religious data in its census. The survey below, the [American Religious Identification Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Religious_Identification_Survey) (ARIS) 2008, was a random digit-dialed telephone [survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistical_survey) of 54,461 American residential households in the [contiguous United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contiguous_United_States). The 1990 sample size was 113,723; 2001 sample size was 50,281.

Adult respondents were asked the [open-ended question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open-ended_question), "What is your religion, if any?". Interviewers did not prompt or offer a suggested list of potential answers. The religion of the spouse or partner was also asked. If the initial answer was "Protestant" or "Christian" further questions were asked to probe which particular denomination. About one-third of the sample was asked more detailed demographic questions.

**Religious Self-Identification of the U.S. Adult Population: 1990, 2001, 2008**[^o768] Figures are not adjusted for refusals to reply; investigators suspect refusals are possibly more representative of "no religion" than any other group.

*Source:ARIS 2008**[^o768]*

## Migration {#Migration}

### Immigration {#Immigration}

#### Foreign-born population {#Foreign-born_population}

*Image: 1990- Growth in share of population that is foreign-born - by country.svg.* In recent decades, the U.S. has grown from having 9% (1990) to 15% (2020) of the population being born abroad.[^o775] The slopes of the tops of the differently-colored columns show the rate of percent increase in foreign-born people living in the respective countries.

*Image: Foreign born population percentage in the US.svg.* Foreign born population percentage in the US over time from 1850 to 2020

As of 2017, an estimated 44,525,458 residents of the United States were foreign-born,[^o778] 13.5% of the country's total population. This demographic includes recent as well as longstanding immigrants; statistically Europeans have resided in the US longer than those from other regions with approximately 66% having arrived prior to 2000.[^o776]

In 2017, out of the U.S. foreign-born population, some 45% (20.7 million) were naturalized citizens, 27% (12.3 million) were lawful permanent residents (including many eligible to become citizens), 6% (2.2 million) were temporary lawful residents, and 23% (10.5 million) were unauthorized immigrants.[^o814] The Pew Research Center estimated that "the number of unauthorized immigrants in the United States reached an all-time high of 14 million in 2023 after two consecutive years of growth".[^o815]

Among current living immigrants to the U.S., the top five countries of birth are Mexico (25% of immigrants), China (6%), India (6%), the Philippines (5%) and [El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Salvador) (3%). Some 13% of current living immigrants come from Europe and Canada, and 10% from the Caribbean.[^o814] Among new arrivals, Asian immigrants have been more numerous than Hispanic immigrants since 2010; in 2017, 37.4% of immigrant arrivals were Asian, and 26.6% were Hispanic.[^o814] Until 2017 and 2018, the United States led the world in [refugee resettlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugee_resettlement) for decades, admitting more refugees than the rest of the world combined.[^o816] From fiscal year 1980 until 2017, 55% of refugees came from Asia, 27% from Europe, 13% from Africa, and 4% from Latin America, fleeing war and persecution.[^o816]

- Net migration rate (2024): 3 migrants/1,000 population.[^o7] Country comparison to the world: 38th[^o7]
- Net migration rate\* (2020–2021): 0.73 migrants/1,000 population.[^o817]

\*(mid-year estimates)

As of 2017, 13.6% (44.4 million) of the population was foreign-born – an increase from 4.7% in 1970 but less than the 1890 record of 14.8%. Some 45% of the foreign-born population were naturalized US citizens. Around 23% (10.3 million) of the foreign-born community is [undocumented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undocumented_immigrants_in_the_United_States), accounting for 3.2% of the total population.[^o814] According to the 2010 census, Latin America and the Caribbean is the largest region-of-birth group, accounting for 53% of the foreign-born population. As of 2018, this region is still the largest source of immigrants to the United States.[^o818][^o819][^o820] In 2018, there were almost 90 million immigrants and U.S. born children of immigrants ([second-generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second-generation_immigrants_in_the_United_States) Americans) in the United States, accounting for 28% of the overall U.S. population.[^o821] In 2018, 1,096,611 immigrants were granted either permanent or temporary legal residence in the United States[^o822]

### Emigration and Expatriation {#Emigration_and_Expatriation}

As of April 2015, the U.S. State Department estimated that 8.7 million American citizens live overseas. Americans living abroad are not counted in the U.S. census unless they are federal government employees or dependents of a federal employee.[^o829] A 2010 paper estimated the number of civilian Americans living abroad to be around 4 million.[^o830] So-called "[accidental Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accidental_American)" are citizens of a country other than the United States who may [also be considered U.S. citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiple_citizenship) or be eligible for [U.S. citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_citizenship) under [specific laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_nationality_law) but are not aware of having such status (or became aware of it only recently).[^o831]

As of 2022, 1.6 million Americans live in [Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico), according to the State Department.[^o832]

## Socioeconomics {#Socioeconomics}

### Income {#Income}

In 2020, the [median household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_household_income) in the United States was around \$67,521, 2.9 percent less than the 2019 median of \$69,560.[^o835] Household and personal income depends on variables such as race, number of [income earners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_earner), educational attainment and marital status.

### Economic class {#Economic_class}

Social classes in the United States lack distinct boundaries and may overlap. Even their existence (when distinguished from economic strata) is controversial. The following table provides a summary of some prominent academic theories on the stratification of American society:

### Unemployment rate (seasonally adjusted) {#Unemployment_rate_(seasonally_adjusted)}

*Image: US unemployment by state.svg.* U.S. unemployment by state in December 2015 (official, or U3 rate)[^o840]\#f4d7d7\#e9afaf\#de8787\#d35f5f\#c83737\#a02c2c\#782121\#501616\#280b0b

*U.S. Unemployment Rate by Year (U3 Rate)*

::: {#o841 .table cue=table name="U.S. Unemployment Rate by Year (U3 Rate)"}

| Year | Unemployment Rate |
|---|---|
|  | \4.2% [^o842] |
|  | \4.2%[^o843] |
|  | \3.5%[^o843] |
|  | \3.5%[^o843] |
|  | \5.4%[^o843] |

|  | \4.9% [^o844] |

|  | \6.2% [^o844] |

:::

The [U6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment#United_States_Bureau_of_Labor_statistics) unemployment rate was 8.6 percent.[^o846] The U6 unemployment rate counts not only people without work seeking full-time employment (the more familiar U3 rate), but also counts "marginally attached workers and those working part-time for economic reasons". Some of these part-time workers counted as employed by U6 could be working as little as an hour a week. And the "marginally attached workers" include those who have become discouraged and stopped looking, but still want to work. The age considered for this calculation is 16 years and over.

Urban Americans have more job opportunities than those in more rural areas. From 2008 to 2018, 72% of the nation's employment growth occurred in cities with more than one million residents, which account for 56% of the overall population.[^o847]

## Generational cohorts {#Generational_cohorts}

A definitive recent study of U.S. generational cohorts was done by Schuman and Scott (2012) in which a broad sample of adults of all ages was asked, "What world events are especially important to you?"[^o849] They found that 33 events were mentioned with great frequency. When the ages of the respondents were correlated with the expressed importance rankings, seven (some indicated eight or nine) distinct cohorts became evident.

Today the following descriptors are frequently used for these cohorts:

*Generational Cohorts*

::: {#o850 .table cue=table name="Generational Cohorts"}

| Generation | Birth years | Notes | Citation |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Lost Generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lost_Generation) | 1883 – 1900 | Came of age during [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I); known for disillusionment and questioning of [traditional values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalist_conservatism). | [^o852] |
| [Greatest Generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greatest_Generation) | 1901 – 1927 | Also called the "[G.I.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G.I.) Generation"; fought in [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II). | [^o854] |
| [Silent Generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silent_Generation) | 1928 – 1945 | Came of age in the [postwar era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_States_%281945–1964%29); includes most who fought in the [Korean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_War). | [^o856] |
| [Baby boomers](wikipedia://en/47127) | 1946 – 1964 | The large generation of children born after World War II to the Greatest and Silent Generations; also called "boomers". | [^o858] |
| [Generation X](wikipedia://en/11973) | 1965 – 1980 | Sometimes called the "baby bust" generation due to declining birth rates after the [baby boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_boom). | [^o860] |
| [Millennials](wikipedia://en/149183) | 1981 – 1996 | Also known as "Generation Y"; often the children of boomers. | [^o862] |
| [Generation Z](wikipedia://en/4015968) | c. 1997 – 2012 | Also known as "zoomers"; often the children of Generation X. | [^o864] |
| [Generation Alpha](wikipedia://en/58852261) | Early 2010s – mid-2020s | First generation fully raised in a digital world; often the children of millennials. | [^o866] |

:::

### U.S. demographic birth cohorts {#U.S._demographic_birth_cohorts}

Subdivided groups are present when peak boom years or inverted peak bust years are present, and may be represented by a [normal](wikipedia://en/21462) or [inverted bell](wikipedia://en/33641902)-shaped curve (rather than a straight curve). The boom subdivided cohorts may be considered as "pre-peak" (including peak year) and "post-peak". The year 1957 was the baby boom peak with 4.3 million births and 122.7 fertility rate. Although post-peak births (such as trailing edge boomers) are in decline, and sometimes referred to as a "bust", there are still a *relatively* large number of births. The dearth-in-birth bust cohorts include those up to the valley birth year, and those including and beyond, leading up to the subsequent normal birth rate. The baby boom began around 1943 to 1946.[^o868]

From the decline in U.S. birth rates starting in 1958 and the introduction of the [birth control pill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_oral_contraceptive_pill) in 1960, the Baby Boomer normal distribution curve is [negatively skewed](wikipedia://en/28212). The trend in birth rates from 1958 to 1961 show a tendency to end late in the decade at approximately 1969, thus returning to levels prior to World War II, with 12 years of rising and 12 years of declining birth rates. Pre-war birth rates were defined as anywhere between 1939 and 1941 by demographers such as the Taeuber's, Philip M. Hauser and [William Fielding Ogburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Fielding_Ogburn).[^o869]

## Mobility {#Mobility}

In 2021, 27.1 million Americans said they were living in a different place than a year before, compared to 29.8 million in 2020. This reflects an 8.4% mover rate, the lowest recorded in more than 70 years.[^o871]

## Education {#Education}

In 2022 the most common level of highest educational attainment among those 25 years old and up (who were civilian and not institutionalized) was completing high school.[^o873]

## Housing {#Housing}

According to the 2020 United States census, the country had a total of 140,498,736 housing units, of which 126,817,580 (90.3%) were occupied and 13,681,156 (9.7%) were classified as being vacant, irrespective of status ("for rent", "rented, not occupied", "for sale only", "sold, not occupied", "for seasonal, recreational, or occasional use", among others). Of the occupied units, 80,051,358 units (63.1%) were owner-occupied, while 46,766,222 units (36.9%) were occupied by tenants.[^o875]

In the 2024 1-year American Community Survey the median number of rooms in a housing unit was 5.5 with the most common amount of rooms for a housing unit being 5 per unit.[^o876] Homes have more excess bedrooms (bedrooms which are not occupied) than in the past compared to 1970 and 1980 but home sizes on average have decreased since the 2010s.[^o877]
